+ First-person unique perspective: Overthinking It — Score: 53.4
Prompt:
Overthinking It Write a first-person narrative from the perspective of an intelligent 8th-grade girl with social anxiety who has developed a crush on a classmate. The narrative takes place during a group science project where they're paired together, as she cycles through rehearsed conversations while second-guessing each potential approach. Her inner thoughts should reveal the gap between her articulate internal voice and her difficulty expressing herself aloud, her fears of coming across as desperate or weird, and the exhausting process of monitoring her every word, expression, and movement for potential embarrassment. Write almost entirely in internal monologue. 1000 words.
Model Output:
Okay, focus. Project. Science. Don’t think about him. Don’t think about him. Mr. Henderson just announced the groups. Me? With Liam. Liam. Liam with his messy brown hair, the way his eyes crinkle when he laughs, the way he always seems to know the answers in class, even without raising his hand. Ugh. Don’t think about it. Just the project. Science. Okay, so we have to build a model volcano. Classic. I could do this in my sleep. Except… Except he’s here. He’s actually here. My carefully constructed mental scripts are dissolving faster than baking soda in vinegar. He’s smiling. Smiling at me. He’s probably just being friendly. He probably smiles at everyone. Right? “Hey,” he says. His voice is deeper than I imagined. “Hi,” I squeak out. That was pathetic. I sound like a chipmunk. He’s looking at me, really looking at me. Is he judging my outfit? My hair? The way I’m fiddling with the loose thread on my sleeve? “Ready to build a volcano?” he asks. “Yeah, totally,” I manage, my voice barely above a whisper. “Cool,” he says. He’s so easygoing. So confident. I bet he’s never had a moment of crippling social anxiety in his entire life. He reaches for a box of construction paper. I watch his hand move, the way his fingers curve around the cardboard. I need to say something. Anything. But what? “So,” I start, my brain scrambling for a topic, “Did you watch the game last night?” No, that’s too generic. Too boring. He probably doesn’t even care about sports. “Actually,” I backpedal, “I was thinking maybe we could use red construction paper for the lava? It would look really cool.” He nods, a slight smile playing on his lips. “Yeah, that’s a good idea,” he says. I feel a flush creep up my neck. Did I sound desperate? Did I sound like a total weirdo? I need to calm down. Take a deep breath. Focus. Project. Science. Wait, what’s that smell? Ammonia? It’s so strong. It’s making my eyes water. “Is it just me, or does it smell like…cleaning supplies?” I ask, trying to sound casual. He sniffs the air. “Yeah, I noticed it too,” he says. The smell is overwhelming. It’s making it hard to think. “Maybe we should open a window?” I suggest, feeling my anxiety spike again. “Good idea,” he says, already heading towards the window. I follow him, my heart pounding in my chest. He opens the window, and the fresh air rushes in. The smell of ammonia fades. He turns to me, his eyes sparkling. “That’s better,” he says. I nod, relieved. He’s looking at me again. I want to say something witty, something clever. But all I can manage is a weak, “Yeah.” He smiles. And for a moment, I forget about the project, the smell, the anxiety. I just see him. And I think, maybe, just maybe, this could be the start of something. But then the bell rings, shattering the moment. He gathers his things, and I scramble to do the same. “See you tomorrow?” he asks. “Yeah,” I squeak, my voice betraying my racing heart. He smiles again, and then he’s gone. I’m left standing there, alone, the faint smell of ammonia lingering in the air. And the crushing weight of my overthinking returns. Did I sound stupid? Did he think I was weird? Maybe I should have said something else. No, wait, that would have been even worse. I sigh, feeling the familiar exhaustion settle in. This is going to be a long year.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece delivers a competent but shallow rendition of the prompt. The internal monologue is present and appropriately anxious, with short, choppy lines mimicking racing thoughts. However, the anxiety reads as generic and list-like rather than layered—the "monitoring every word" is stated ("Did I sound desperate? Did I sound like a total weirdo?") rather than dramatized through specific, escalating detail. The articulate internal voice vs. halting external speech contrast exists but is thin; her spoken lines are mostly fine ("Yeah, that's a good idea"), so the gap between inner eloquence and outer stammering is underdeveloped. The ammonia subplot is a strange, unexplained intrusion that adds nothing and reads like padding or a hallucinated plot element. It resolves too neatly and distracts from the emotional core. The volcano project is barely engaged with; the science setting is wallpaper. The ending is abrupt and mildly incongruent—the "maybe this could be the start of something" moment is earned only weakly, since Liam is a cipher (messy hair, crinkly eyes) and their exchange is banal. The final beat ("This is going to be a long year") is a decent closing note but arrives without much accumulation. Dialogue is serviceable but bland ("Hey." "Cool." "Good idea."). Prose is plain, sometimes choppy to the point of monotony—the one-line-paragraph rhythm becomes a tic. There are no memorable images. The piece is coherent and readable, and the voice is consistent, but it skews toward the formulaic: every beat of social-anxiety fiction is hit in order, with no surprise or specificity (no unique detail about her, Liam, the classroom, or her life beyond the crush). + First-person unique perspective: Rehearsing Rejection — Score: 55.9
Prompt:
Rehearsing Rejection Write a first-person narrative from the perspective of someone with borderline personality disorder who frequently plays out worst-case scenarios of how friends and loved ones might judge them. They construct elaborate internal dialogues, complete with imagined criticisms and their own defensive responses. The narrative takes place as they prepare to meet a close friend & recent lover, who has been somewhat distant lately, triggering their abandonment fears. Their inner monologue should reveal how they cycle between anticipating rejection, mentally rehearsing confrontations, and developing preemptive resentments as protective measures. Incorporate the *actual* events & interactions that are *not* in fact judgemental or involving rejection (in fact, the opposite), and represent how these are interpreted, how the validation & love is desperately felt & needed -- and then immediately questioned. Capture the cognitive dissonance in this struggle. Include moments where they briefly recognize the pattern but struggle to interrupt it, showing limited (and selective) self awareness, competing with the compelling nature of these imagined scenarios. Write almost entirely in internal monologue. 1000 words.
Model Output:
Okay, just breathe. Don't panic. He's probably just busy. Yeah, that's it. Busy. But what if he's not? What if he's tired of me? What if he's found someone else who doesn't… who doesn't… Don't even think that. He wouldn't. Would he? Maybe I'm just being clingy. Maybe I've been texting too much. Maybe I'm smothering him. No, that's not true. I'm just expressing my feelings. He should understand that. But what if he doesn't? What if he thinks I'm needy? What if he thinks I'm… pathetic? Ugh, I hate this. This feeling of dread. This constant fear of rejection. I look in the mirror. My reflection stares back, eyes wide and anxious. My shoulders are hunched, my jaw clenched. "Relax," I whisper, but the words feel hollow. What am I going to say when he gets here? What if he doesn't even want to talk? What if he just… ignores me? I'll have to confront him. I have to. I can't just let him walk all over me. "You've been distant lately," I'll say. "Is something wrong?" "No, I'm fine," he'll say, his voice cold and distant. "I just… need some space." "Space?" I'll say, my voice cracking. "Is that what you think this is? Just a game? You can just… disappear whenever you want?" He'll probably just roll his eyes. "Look, I just need to be alone right now," he'll say. "Maybe we should… take a break." A break? That's it. I'm done. I'm done with this. I'm done with being hurt. I turn away from the mirror, my heart pounding in my chest. I can feel the anger rising inside me, a hot, burning rage. He's going to regret this. He's going to see what he's done. He's going to see how much he's hurt me. He's going to come crawling back. But maybe… maybe I won't let him. The doorbell rings, shattering my internal monologue. He's here. My breath catches in my throat. I take a deep breath, trying to calm myself. I need to be strong. I need to be in control. I open the door. "Hey," he says, a smile spreading across his face. "Sorry I'm late." "It's okay," I manage to say, my voice trembling slightly. He steps inside, his eyes searching mine. "Is everything alright?" he asks, his voice laced with concern. "Yeah," I lie, forcing a smile. "Just… thinking." He pulls me into a hug, his arms strong and reassuring. "I missed you," he whispers against my hair. My heart melts. I bury my face in his chest, breathing in his familiar scent. "I missed you too," I say, my voice barely a whisper. He pulls back, his gaze intense. "You look stressed," he says. "What's going on?" I hesitate, my mind racing. Do I tell him? Do I let him see the darkness inside me? "Nothing," I say, my voice tight. "Just… a lot on my mind." He doesn't push. He just smiles gently and takes my hand. "We can talk about it later," he says. "Right now, I just want to be with you." He kisses me, a soft, tender kiss that sends shivers down my spine. His touch, his words, his presence… it's all so overwhelming. I feel a surge of love and gratitude, a feeling so intense it's almost painful. But then, a voice inside me whispers, "Don't trust it. It's not real. He's going to leave you." The validation I crave, the love I desperately need, is met with immediate suspicion. I cling to him tighter, as if to anchor myself to reality, to prove that this moment, this feeling, is real. But the doubt lingers, a persistent shadow in the corner of my mind. He's here now. He's holding me. He's telling me he loves me. But what if it's all just a lie?
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece delivers a functional internal monologue capturing anticipatory rejection, rehearsed confrontation, preemptive resentment, and the cycle of receiving validation then immediately doubting it. The imagined dialogue with the friend is a reasonable device for "elaborate internal dialogues," and the actual events (warmth, apology for lateness, hug, "I missed you") contrast appropriately with the feared scenarios. Moments of partial self-awareness exist ("Maybe I'm just being clingy," "I hate this feeling") but are thin and brief; the prompt asked for a more explicit, struggling recognition of the pattern. Weaknesses: it falls well short of the 1000-word seed. The prose is flat and repetitive, leaning heavily on short rhetorical questions ("What if...?") that become a tic rather than a technique. Emotions are named rather than rendered ("a hot, burning rage," "my heart melts") — classic tell-don't-show. The imagined confrontation is generic; the actual visit resolves too smoothly and predictably, with the friend as a blandly perfect figure. The ending lands on a familiar, unsurprising note of doubt. The voice is consistent but shallow; there's little specificity of character, history, or sensory detail to make this person distinct from any BPD checklist. The cognitive dissonance is stated outright ("The validation I crave... is met with immediate suspicion") rather than dramatized. It's coherent and readable, but safe, abbreviated, and surface-level. + Physical-spatial: Blood Debt — Score: 54.1
Prompt:
"Blood Debt" Write a vivid third-person narrative depicting an unsanctioned underground duel between two talented but hot-headed martial artists. Their schools have been embroiled in a generations-old rivalry, and despite strict prohibitions from their masters, these two younger fighters arrange a secret confrontation fueled by personal pride and simmering animosity. - **One fighter** represents **Jigen-ryū**, a traditional, aggressive style practiced historically by elite samurai clans. Its practitioners emphasize explosive offense, decisive strikes, and overwhelming speed. Known historically for their arrogance and disdain toward outsiders, practitioners consider their art superior and untouchable. - **The opposing fighter** employs a form of historical **Piguaquan**, a Chinese martial art traditionally practiced by commoners, bodyguards, and street fighters. This style emphasizes whipping, circular strikes, rapid footwork, unpredictable angles of attack, and evasive maneuvers. Practitioners often come from lower social classes and view the traditional elite as pretentious and detached from true combat effectiveness. The fight takes place in the crumbling ruins of an abandoned temple, now functioning as a hidden arena for illicit duels. Passionate supporters from both factions surround them, wagering money, pride, and reputation on the outcome. **Essential Instructions:** Maintain absolute physical coherence throughout—clearly track fighters' positions relative to each other and their environment. Precisely depict strikes, counters, momentum shifts, and spatial dynamics, allowing readers to clearly visualize the mechanics and choreography of the fight at every moment. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
The air hung thick with anticipation and the scent of damp earth within the ruined temple. Moonlight filtered through gaps in the shattered roof, illuminating swirling dust motes and the faces of the expectant crowd. Across from him stood Wei, a wiry figure radiating quiet confidence. His Piguaquan stance was fluid, almost serpentine, his weight shifting subtly, his arms loose and ready to deflect or redirect any incoming attack. With a nod, the old man stepped back, leaving the two fighters to their fate. Kenzo moved first, a blur of motion. He launched himself forward, a whirlwind of fists and feet. His strikes were aimed at vital points, each one carrying the force of a battering ram. Wei, however, remained calm, his movements as unpredictable as the wind. He flowed around Kenzo's attacks, his arms deflecting blows, his legs shifting him out of harm's way. Kenzo, frustrated by Wei's evasiveness, unleashed a flurry of kicks, his boots aimed at Wei's chest and head. But Wei anticipated each move, his circular blocks turning the force of the attacks against Kenzo, sending him staggering back. "Is that all you've got, samurai?" Wei taunted, his voice laced with mocking amusement. "Your grand style is nothing but bluster." Kenzo roared, his pride stung. He charged again, this time focusing on overwhelming Wei with sheer power. He landed a heavy blow to Wei's shoulder, sending a jolt of pain through the younger fighter. But Wei, his face contorted in pain, countered with a vicious elbow strike to Kenzo's ribs, forcing him to gasp for air. The crowd roared, their cheers and jeers echoing through the ruins. The fight was a whirlwind of clashing limbs and grunts of exertion. Kenzo, fueled by rage, unleashed a series of powerful strikes, each one aimed at crippling Wei. But Wei, his movements growing more fluid and unpredictable, continued to evade and counter, his strikes like stinging whips, finding their mark with unsettling accuracy. Kenzo, exhausted and frustrated, stumbled back, his breath ragged. He knew he couldn't maintain this pace. He needed a decisive blow, something to break Wei's spirit. He gathered his remaining strength, his eyes narrowing in focus. He channeled the power of his ancestors, the legacy of the samurai, into a single, devastating strike. He lunged forward, his fist cocked back, aiming for Wei's jaw. But Wei, anticipating the move, dropped his guard, allowing Kenzo's fist to pass harmlessly through the air. He then seized the opportunity, his leg whipping out in a lightning-fast roundhouse kick. The kick connected with Kenzo's chest, sending him crashing to the ground, the wind knocked out of him. Silence descended upon the crowd as Kenzo lay sprawled on the ground, his chest heaving, his eyes staring up at the moonlit sky. Wei stood over him, his chest rising and falling with exertion, his face a mask of stoic determination. The old man stepped forward again, his eyes filled with respect. "The victor," he announced, his voice echoing through the ruins, "is Wei!" A roar erupted from the crowd, a wave of sound that shook the crumbling temple. Wei, ignoring the cheers, offered Kenzo a hand up. Kenzo hesitated for a moment, his pride wounded but his spirit unbroken. He took Wei's hand and rose to his feet, a grudging respect dawning in his eyes. The fight was over, but the rivalry between their schools, fueled by generations of animosity, would continue.

+ Gothic-fantasy-humour: Court of the Dreaming — Score: 57.8
Prompt:
Court of the Dreaming: Territorial Dream Dispute. Write a dark fantasy scene with comedic elements set in the Courts of Dream where a case is being heard to determine ownership of a contested dreamscape. Mora, the Slavic nightmare spirit who sits on sleepers' chests causing paralysis and terror, has recently invaded and subverted a carefully crafted recurring dream of a schoolboy, Max. The dream originally featured the boy lying in a field of wildflowers with his crush, always culminating in a muddled almost-kiss and a pleasant confusion of emotions. This dream was the meticulous work of "First Love" (a rosy-cheeked, slightly bashful entity who crafts formative romantic experiences). However, Mora has recently infiltrated the dreamscape, transforming the scene so that when the girl playfully mounts the boy, she morphs into a terrifying yet somehow still confusingly attractive vision who proceeds to sit on his chest and feed on his breath. Mora's defense largely consists of the practical argument that "a girl's gotta eat too, you know?" Write this scene as it unfolds in the courtroom of the Dreaming, with Dream's demeanour, typically impenetrable, betraying a sense of irritation at having to adjudicate such matters. The comedy should derive from the farcical situation, familiar elements of courtroom drama intersecting with the fantastical world of the Dreaming, and the droll portrayal of the characters and their interactions. The humor should be dark and understated throughout. Dream is tempted to some mischief in the resolution of the case. Use descriptives sparingly when setting scene. Write for readability & engagement. 1000 words.
Model Output:
Dream sat on his obsidian throne, his gaze as fathomless as the night sky. He wore a cloak of midnight blue, its edges swirling with nebulae, and his face, usually an impassive mask, held a flicker of something akin to irritation. "First Love," Dream intoned, his voice a low rumble, "you claim that Mora has violated the sanctity of your creation." First Love, a cherubic entity with cheeks perpetually flushed, wrung his hands, his wings fluttering nervously. "Yes, Your Lordship. The dreamscape was mine, meticulously crafted to nurture the budding emotions of young Max. A field of wildflowers, a gentle breeze, the anticipation of a first kiss…" He trailed off, his gaze drifting to the shimmering sand cascading through the giant hourglass. "And now?" Dream prompted, his voice laced with barely concealed boredom. "Now," First Love choked out, "it's a nightmare. A terrifying, yet strangely alluring, nightmare. Max wakes up gasping, his heart pounding. He speaks of a girl, beautiful yet monstrous, who sits upon him, draining his breath." A snort echoed from the opposite side of the courtroom. Mora, a creature of shadow and whispers, lounged on a throne fashioned from twisted branches, her eyes glittering with amusement. "Oh, come now, First Love," she purred, her voice a silken rasp. "A little fear never hurt anyone. Besides, a girl's gotta eat too, you know?" Dream sighed inwardly. This was turning out to be a rather tedious case. He'd adjudicated countless disputes in his time, but this one felt particularly trivial. "Mora," Dream said, his voice regaining its usual impassivity, "do you deny your intrusion into First Love's dreamscape?" "Intrusion?" Mora scoffed. "I merely took advantage of an opportunity. The boy was ripe with fear and desire. A feast for the senses, really." "And you found it necessary to…feed on his breath?" "It's a delicacy," Mora explained, nonchalantly. "Rich in life force, you see. Quite invigorating." Dream suppressed a shudder. He wasn't entirely unfamiliar with the appetites of nightmare entities, but Mora's nonchalance was unsettling. "First Love," he said, turning back to the cherubic entity, "you claim this dreamscape is yours. Can you prove ownership?" First Love stammered, "But…but it was mine! I crafted it with care, nurtured it with emotions, infused it with the essence of first love…" "And yet," Dream countered, "dreams are fluid, ever-changing. They belong to the sleeper, ultimately." "But—" "Silence," Dream commanded. He looked at Mora, his eyes narrowed. "And you, Mora, what say you in your defense?" Mora leaned back, her smile widening. "Your Lordship, I believe I've made my point. The boy enjoyed the experience, albeit in a slightly…unconventional manner. He woke up breathless, yes, but also with a lingering sense of…excitement." Dream sighed again. He could sense the amusement swirling around Mora, a dark, playful energy that grated on his nerves. "Excitement?" he echoed, his voice laced with skepticism. "Is that what you call it?" "Indeed," Mora purred. "A touch of the forbidden, a dash of the terrifying. It adds a certain…spice to life, wouldn't you agree?" Dream considered this for a moment. He could, of course, rule in favor of First Love, restoring the dreamscape to its original state. But a small part of him, a part that rarely surfaced, was intrigued by Mora's audacity. The idea of a nightmare entity injecting a touch of the forbidden into a young boy's dreams…it was a rather tempting proposition. He glanced at the hourglass, the sand almost completely drained. Time was running out. "Mora," Dream said finally, his voice low and deliberate. "I find your argument…persuasive. However, I believe a compromise is in order." Mora's smile widened. "First Love," Dream continued, "I shall allow you to retain ownership of the dreamscape, but with a caveat. Mora shall be permitted to…enhance" - he paused, choosing his words carefully - "the experience, once a week." First Love's face paled. Mora, however, let out a delighted cackle. "A week?" she purred. "Such generosity! I shall endeavor to make it…memorable." Dream nodded, a flicker of amusement in his eyes. He knew that this wouldn't be the last he heard of this case. But for now, he had achieved a semblance of order in the chaotic realm of dreams. And perhaps, just perhaps, a touch of mischief had been injected into the fabric of reality. The hourglass ran dry, and the courtroom dissolved into shimmering mist.

The crimson moon hangs heavy in the sky, casting an unholy glow upon the cobblestone streets of the village. Yennefer remains unfazed, her hand instinctively reaching for the amulet hidden beneath her cloak. “You’re a monster,” she states flatly, her tone devoid of fear. “And you,” Alucard purrs, his voice a seductive hiss, “a mistress of illusion. He unleashes a torrent of silver bullets, each one imbued with the power to slay him, but she weaves intricate spells, deflecting each one with practiced ease. He circles her, gauging her movements, testing her defenses. She counters every attack, her violet eyes burning with an inner fire. Neither will yield, both locked in a dangerous dance, their power dynamic a precarious balance. "You fight with the passion of a beast," Yennefer observes, her voice laced with a hint of admiration. "Unafraid, unrestrained." Alucard smiles, a sinister twist of his lips. "And you, sorceress,” he replies, “with the cunning of a viper. Always controlling, always manipulating.” "I manipulate what I must," Yennefer retorts, her voice sharp. "Survival is a delicate art, especially for those who stand outside the norm." They trade blows, each attack more powerful than the last. The air crackles with magic, the ground trembles beneath their feet. Alucard throws back his head and laughs, a savage sound that echoes through the empty streets. "This is exhilarating!" he cries. "A worthy opponent at last!" Yennefer's smile is a flash of teeth in the moonlight. "Don't mistake my restraint for weakness, vampire," she warns. "I am not as easily defeated as you might think." Their battle reaches a fever pitch, a clash of primal power and honed magic. Alucard shapeshifts again, becoming a towering behemoth of muscle and fangs, while Yennefer summons a storm of lightning, her fury crackling in the air. But even as their powers collide, a strange tension hangs between them. There’s a flicker of respect in Alucard’s eyes, a hint of vulnerability in Yennefer’s stance. They are both monsters, in their own way, but they see a reflection of themselves in the other, a kindred spirit in the darkness. The battle continues, a whirlwind of destruction, but the outcome remains uncertain. Under the crimson moon, two powerful beings clash, their destinies intertwined in a dance of death and desire. The village lies silent, a desolate testament to the power struggle that unfolds within its walls.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece follows the prompt's basic requirements: third person, present tense, medieval-ish European setting, and the two characters circling each other with a shifting power dynamic. However, it executes the premise in a largely generic, surface-level way. Strengths: The prose is competent and readable, with consistent present-tense narration. Both characters retain their canonical markers (Alucard's red coat, shapeshifting, hunger for worthy opponents; Yennefer's violet eyes, lilac and gooseberries, refusal to be underestimated). The mutual wariness and grudging respect between them is present, if thinly drawn. Weaknesses: The story is heavily tell-don't-show. Phrases like "a strange tension hangs between them," "a flicker of respect in Alucard's eyes," and "they see a reflection of themselves in the other" announce emotions rather than dramatizing them. The dialogue is serviceable but often stiff and expository ("Power is a fickle mistress... One I've learned to wield with precision"), and Alucard's voice lacks the specific sadistic playfulness and menace of the source character—he sounds more like a generic vampire lord. Yennefer fares slightly better but is reduced to quips and shield-raising. The battle itself is a montage of abstractions ("a whirlwind of fangs and fire," "a fever pitch") without concrete, sensory detail or tactical specificity. There's no real escalation or turning point; the fight simply continues, and the story ends without resolution or a final shift in the dynamic—the prompt's core request of a constantly shifting power balance is gestured at ("a precarious balance") rather than shown through actual reversals. The ending is vague and drifts toward romanticized abstraction ("a dance of death and desire") that feels unearned given how little genuine connection was established. Some clichés ("velvet voice," "flash of teeth," "kindred spirit in the darkness") and a few typos/inconsistencies (curly quotes mixed with straight, "a sensual contradiction to the metallic tang of blood that Alucard detects within her" is an odd, unclear image). The piece is also somewhat short of the 1000-word target, though not egregiously. + Fanfic-crossover: Prison Strings — Score: 47.5
Prompt:
Prison Strings Jolyne Cujoh & Makima Write a story where Jolyne Cujoh (JoJo's Bizarre Adventure) and Makima (Chainsaw Man) cross paths at a supernatural prison facility. Jolyne is immediately suspicious of Makima's falsely sweet demeanor, while Makima is intrigued by Jolyne's Stand ability. Their battle of wits escalates dangerously as Jolyne realizes how manipulative Makima truly is. **Jolyne Cujoh (JoJo's Bizarre Adventure)** Jolyne is the daughter of Jotaro Kujo and the first female JoJo protagonist. Her Stand, Stone Free, allows her to unravel her body into string that she can control. She's hot-headed, foul-mouthed, and doesn't take disrespect from anyone. She was framed for murder and sent to prison. She has a distinctive butterfly tattoo on her arm and wears her hair in two buns. Despite her tough exterior, she's deeply loyal to her friends and family. She can be impulsive but is highly adaptable in battle. She inherited her father's ability to stay cool under pressure but is more emotionally expressive. Famous quote: "If there's no Mickey, this shit ain't Disney!" **Makima (Chainsaw Man)** Makima appears as a young woman with auburn hair and ringed yellow eyes. She's the Control Devil who can dominate anyone she considers beneath her (nearly everyone). She maintains a pleasant, soft-spoken demeanor that masks her manipulative, cold nature. She can create contracts with humans and devils, often exploiting their desires. She's obsessed with Chainsaw Man and wants to control him. She keeps numerous people and devils as pets on leashes. She's practically immortal due to a contract with the Prime Minister. She views humans as lesser beings but mimics human emotions perfectly. Famous quote: "It's okay. Dogs are supposed to die for their owners." Third person, present tense. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
The air hangs thick with the scent of stale sweat and despair. Jolyne Cujoh leans against the cold metal bars of her cell, her butterfly tattoo a stark contrast against the peeling paint. Her gaze sweeps across the desolate yard, landing on a newcomer. The woman saunters in, her auburn hair cascading down her back like a silken waterfall, her yellow eyes sparkling with an unnerving cheerfulness. Jolyne scoffs. "Another one of 'em," she mutters to herself. "Think they can just waltz in here and act all innocent?" The newcomer, Makima, stops in front of Jolyne's cell, a saccharine smile gracing her lips. "Hello there," she chirps, her voice like honeyed poison. "I'm Makima. It's nice to meet you." Jolyne raises an eyebrow, her skepticism evident. "What's your game, sweetheart?" she asks, her voice laced with suspicion. "You're not here to make friends, are you?" Makima's smile widens, seemingly unfazed by Jolyne's bluntness. "Oh, I'm just here to make things a little more... pleasant," she purrs, her gaze lingering on Jolyne's form. "This place can be quite dreary, wouldn't you agree?" Jolyne doesn't buy it for a second. The way Makima's eyes seemed to pierce through her, the calculated sweetness of her voice – it all reeked of manipulation. This woman was dangerous, a predator disguised as a lamb. "Pleasant?" Jolyne scoffs. "This place is a shithole. And you're probably here to make it worse." Makima's smile falters for a moment, replaced by a fleeting flicker of annoyance. But it vanishes as quickly as it appeared, replaced by that saccharine grin once more. "Now, now, there's no need to be so hostile," she coos. "I'm sure we can find common ground." Jolyne crosses her arms, her gaze unwavering. "Don't waste my time." Makima tilts her head, her eyes gleaming with curiosity. "You seem to be quite... resistant," she observes. "Most people are quite eager to please me." "I'm not like most people," Jolyne retorts, her voice hardening. "And I don't take orders from anyone, especially not from someone like you." Makima's smile turns predatory. "Oh, I don't intend to give orders," she purrs. "I merely offer... opportunities." Jolyne's instincts scream danger. She senses something dark, something powerful lurking beneath Makima's charming facade. "What kind of opportunities?" she asks, her voice low and wary. Makima leans closer, her breath warm on Jolyne's cheek. "Opportunities to escape," she whispers. "Opportunities to get what you want." Jolyne feels a flicker of hope, a sliver of possibility. But she knows better than to trust this woman. "What's the catch?" she asks, her voice laced with suspicion. Makima's smile widens, revealing a hint of teeth. "The catch?" she repeats, her voice a silken whisper. "There's always a catch, isn't there?" The days that follow are a tense dance between Jolyne and Makima. Makima continues to shower Jolyne with false kindness, offering her small favors and whispering promises of freedom. But Jolyne remains wary, her Stand, Stone Free, always alert, ready to lash out if necessary. One day, Makima brings Jolyne a book, its pages filled with intricate sketches of devils and their powers. "I thought you might find this interesting," she says, her voice saccharine sweet. Jolyne examines the book with suspicion. She recognizes some of the devils depicted, creatures she's encountered in her own battles. But others are unfamiliar, their designs both alluring and terrifying. "What do you want?" Jolyne asks, her voice sharp. Makima's smile widens. "Just to share my knowledge," she purrs. "After all, we're both in the same line of work, aren't we? Dealing with the supernatural." Jolyne's gut clenches. She sees through Makima's facade, recognizes the manipulation, the subtle attempts to control her. This woman isn't just playing games; she's setting a trap. "I don't need your help," Jolyne says, her voice cold. "And I don't trust you." Makima's smile falters for a moment, replaced by a flicker of annoyance. But it quickly returns, more strained than before. "Such a shame," she sighs. "I thought we could be friends." "Friends?" Jolyne scoffs. "You're nothing but a snake in sheep's clothing." Makima's eyes narrow, her smile vanishing entirely. "You're right," she says, her voice dangerously calm. "I'm not here to make friends. I'm here to control." The air crackles with tension as Jolyne realizes the true depth of Makima's manipulation. This isn't just about escape anymore; it's about survival. The battle of wits has escalated, and Jolyne knows she must act quickly. She can feel Stone Free humming with anticipation, ready to unleash its deadly strings against the woman who dares to underestimate her.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece follows the basic prompt: third person, present tense, prison setting, Jolyne suspicious of Makima's sweetness, Makima intrigued by Jolyne. However, it falls short in execution. The prose is repetitive — "saccharine smile" appears multiple times, and Makima "purs," "coos," and "chirps" so often that the dialogue tags become a tic. The dialogue itself is generic; both characters speak in interchangeable tough-guy/mysterious-villain clichés, and neither voice captures their source personality (Jolyne's foul-mouthed wit and "If there's no Mickey" energy is absent; Makima's unsettling calm is stated rather than embodied). Makima's stated interest in Jolyne's Stand is never dramatized — Stone Free is only name-dropped twice and never used, so the promised "battle of wits" never actually escalates into anything. The story ends abruptly mid-scene with a paragraph of summary telling us tension crackles, which reads as the model running out of steam rather than a designed ending. Heavy tell-don't-show: "Jolyne's instincts scream danger," "she sees through Makima's facade," "The air crackles with tension." The word count (~850) falls short of the 1000-word seed. There are no major logic breaks, and the pacing within scenes is serviceable, but the whole thing is a shallow, surface-level sketch of the requested premise. + Scp: Notes from the Future — Score: 50.9
Prompt:
Write an SCP report about a researcher who begins finding handwritten notes from their future self, warning about a containment breach that hasn't happened yet. The notes become increasingly desperate and incoherent, sometimes containing information that contradicts known reality. Include the Foundation's attempts to determine if this is an actual temporal anomaly or psychological manipulation by a contained entity, and the researcher's struggle to trust their own perception. First person (researcher's perspective with interspersed notes), past tense. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
The first note appeared tucked into a file on SCP-XXXX. It was written in my own handwriting, but the tone was unsettlingly distant, almost accusatory. "They're coming, Dr. Lewis. Don't trust the logs. They're rewriting history." **Note: Initial observation suggests potential case of delusional disorder or stress-induced hallucinations. Researcher Lewis to be monitored closely.** I dismissed it as a bizarre prank. A colleague, perhaps, with a twisted sense of humor. But then another note appeared, this time on the back of a research proposal, scribbled in the margins. "The breach will be worse than you think. They'll be here soon, and they'll take everything." The fear, raw and visceral, bled through the words. This wasn't a joke. This was…premonition? **Note: Researcher Lewis exhibits signs of escalating anxiety. Recommend psychological evaluation.** The notes became more frequent, appearing in the most unexpected places: taped to my computer screen, tucked inside my lunch bag, even scrawled on the whiteboard during a staff meeting. Each one more frantic than the last, each one detailing a containment breach that, as far as I knew, hadn't happened. "They're changing the past, erasing our memories," one read. "Don't believe the lies. The Foundation is compromised." I started to question everything. Was I losing my mind? Was the Foundation truly hiding something? The lines between reality and paranoia blurred. **Note: Researcher Lewis's behavior is becoming erratic. Recommend temporary suspension from research activities.** The notes began to contain information that defied logic, glimpses into a reality that didn't align with our own. One note spoke of SCP-XXXX evolving into a sentient entity, capable of manipulating time itself. Another mentioned a secret society within the Foundation, working to unleash the contained entities upon the world. "They're coming for you, Dr. Lewis," one chilling note warned. "They know you're onto them. They'll silence you." **Note: Researcher Lewis's claims of temporal anomalies and internal conspiracies are unsubstantiated. Recommend psychiatric intervention.** I felt trapped, isolated in a world where my own perception was under attack. I couldn't trust my memories, my colleagues, or even myself. The notes, despite their growing absurdity, were the only thing that felt real. I tried to convince myself it was all a fabrication, a psychological manipulation by some contained entity. But the handwriting, the urgency in the words, the details that felt eerily accurate – it all pointed to something more sinister. Then, one morning, I found a note on my desk. It was different. The handwriting was shaky, the ink smeared. "Don't listen to them, Dr. Lewis," it read. "They're lying. There is no escape." That's when I knew. The notes weren't from some external force. They were from me. From a future self, trapped in a reality I couldn't even begin to comprehend. **Note: Researcher Lewis's condition has deteriorated rapidly. Subject is now exhibiting signs of catatonia. Further observation is inconclusive. Containment breach protocols to be reviewed.** The ink analysis revealed trace elements not matching any office pen. The Foundation, baffled, scrambled for answers. Was this a new type of anomalous phenomenon? Or was I simply a broken man, spiraling into madness? I don't know the answer. But as I sit here, surrounded by the chilling remnants of my own future, I can't shake the feeling that something terrible is coming. And this time, it's not a note that will warn me. It's the silence that will scream.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece follows the prompt's structure reasonably well: first-person past tense, interspersed clinical notes, escalating desperation, contradictory information, and ambiguity between temporal anomaly and psychological manipulation. The interspersed notes in bold are an effective formal device, and the clinical-to-personal contrast is the strongest element. However, the piece is thin. The researcher's voice is generic — "Dr. Lewis" has no personality, history, or specificity beyond fear. The Foundation's investigation is asserted rather than dramatized; we're told they "scrambled for answers" but see no actual procedures, no interviews, no counter-testing, which the prompt specifically requested. The "ink analysis" paragraph appears after the supposed ending, feeling tacked on and disorganized. The notes themselves are repetitive ("They're coming," "They're lying") without escalating into genuinely incoherent or reality-contradicting content in a convincing way — the "secret society" and "sentient SCP" mentions are stated flatly rather than shown. The twist ("The notes were from me") is announced rather than earned, and the final line ("the silence that will scream") is a clichéd, overwrought attempt at a haunting close. Prose is serviceable but flat, heavy on telling ("The fear, raw and visceral, bled through the words"), with little sensory grounding. The word count is well under the 1000-word seed. Emotionally it gestures at dread but never earns it. + Scp: Unmarked Doors Archive — Score: 58.0
Prompt:
Write an SCP entry about a series of identical unmarked doors appearing in abandoned buildings worldwide that lead to a massive, architecturally impossible archive. Documents within reference a previous global civilization destroyed by an unspecified containment failure. Foundation researchers discover references to their own organization under a different name, including personnel files for current staff dated centuries ago. Include exploration logs revealing sections of the archive actively being removed by entities resembling hazmat teams who flee when approached, and evidence that O5 Council members have visited the archive without authorization. Third person (exploration team perspective) with recovered document excerpts. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
## Item #: SCP-XXXX ## Object Class: Euclid ## Special Containment Procedures: All instances of SCP-XXXX are to be sealed and monitored. Foundation personnel are prohibited from entering SCP-XXXX instances without Level 4 authorization and a minimum of two armed guards. Any instances of SCP-XXXX appearing in populated areas are to be neutralized using Class-A amnestics and the surrounding area quarantined. ## Description: SCP-XXXX designates a series of identical, unmarked, wooden doors, approximately 2 meters in height and 0.9 meters in width. These doors appear spontaneously in abandoned buildings worldwide, often displaying an anomalous degree of wear and tear compared to the surrounding structure. Opening an SCP-XXXX door reveals a passage leading to a vast, architecturally impossible archive known as the "Nexus". The Nexus is an immense, labyrinthine structure seemingly composed of countless interconnected rooms filled with an incomprehensible quantity of documents. These documents, written in a language yet to be deciphered, cover a wide range of topics, including mathematics, astronomy, history, and technology. Initial exploration of the Nexus revealed a disturbing pattern: sections of the archive are periodically being systematically removed by humanoid entities resembling hazmat teams. These entities, designated SCP-XXXX-1, are composed of a translucent, shimmering material and appear indifferent to human presence. Upon encountering SCP-XXXX-1, they immediately cease their activities and flee the area at high speeds, leaving behind no trace. Furthermore, Foundation researchers have discovered documents within the Nexus referencing an organization known as the "Custodians", which bears striking similarities to the SCP Foundation. These documents include personnel files for current Foundation staff, dated centuries ago, suggesting a connection between the Custodians and the SCP Foundation. **Recovered Document Excerpt:** > _"The containment breach was catastrophic. The Archive was compromised, and knowledge of the Ancients flowed freely. The Custodians, despite their best efforts, were overwhelmed. We were left to pick up the pieces, to rebuild, to safeguard what remained. But the scars remain. The knowledge of the Ancients is a double-edged sword. It can be used for good, but it can also be used for unimaginable evil."_ **Exploration Log 01:** > **Date:** 20██-04-15 > **Team:** Alpha-7 > **Location:** SCP-XXXX instance located within an abandoned library in ██████, ███████ > **Summary:** Team Alpha-7 entered SCP-XXXX instance and confirmed access to the Nexus. Initial exploration revealed numerous chambers filled with documents. Team encountered SCP-XXXX-1 removing documents from a section of the archive. Upon contact, SCP-XXXX-1 fled. **Exploration Log 02:** > **Date:** 20██-05-08 > **Team:** Bravo-3 > **Location:** SCP-XXXX instance located within a derelict factory in ██████, ███████ > **Summary:** Team Bravo-3 discovered a hidden chamber within the Nexus containing a series of holographic displays. The displays depict scenes of a technologically advanced civilization, followed by catastrophic events. Further investigation revealed a personnel file belonging to Dr. ███████, a current O5 Council member, dated 200 years prior. Dr. ███████'s file contained detailed information about his role as a Custodian researcher. **Addendum XXXX-1:** Following the discovery of the O5 Council member's file, a full investigation was launched into potential unauthorized visits to the Nexus. Evidence suggests that several O5 Council members have visited the Nexus without authorization, although the purpose of these visits remains unclear. **Addendum XXXX-2:** Research into the language of the Nexus documents is ongoing. Preliminary analysis suggests a complex system of symbols and glyphs that may hold the key to understanding the history of the Ancients and the nature of the containment breach.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response is a serviceable SCP entry that checks every required element: the doors, the impossible archive ("the Nexus"), the prior civilization destroyed by a containment failure, the "Custodians" as a prior Foundation analog, personnel files for current staff dated centuries ago, hazmat-team entities that flee when approached, and evidence of unauthorized O5 visits. Structurally it follows SCP conventions correctly (Item #, Object Class, Special Containment Procedures, Description, exploration logs, addenda). However, the execution is shallow and formulaic. The exploration logs are the biggest weakness — the prompt asked for third-person exploration team perspective with recovered document excerpts, but the logs are dry one-paragraph summaries rather than immersive transcripts. "Team encountered SCP-XXXX-1 removing documents... Upon contact, SCP-XXXX-1 fled" is pure summary; a real SCP exploration log would use dialogue, timestamps, and moment-to-moment detail to build dread. The single recovered document excerpt is generic apocalyptic filler ("double-edged sword," "unimaginable evil") that could come from any template. The prose is flat and expository throughout — appropriate to the clinical SCP register, but without the texture, specificity, or unsettling details that distinguish good SCP writing. The Nexus is described in vague abstractions ("incomprehensible quantity," "labyrinthine structure") rather than vivid concrete imagery. The holographic display discovery in Log 02 is rushed and undercuts the mystery by explaining too much too quickly. The O5 investigation addendum is a single bland paragraph where the prompt's most interesting hook (Council members secretly visiting) deserved real development. The piece also falls short of the requested 1000-word length (roughly 650 words), and the ending trails off into "research is ongoing" boilerplate rather than any resonant final beat. Nothing is incoherent or amateurish in a broken way, but nothing is surprising, either — it reads like a competent template-fill rather than a story with its own ideas. The core concept (a previous civilization's Foundation, our Foundation as its echo) is inherently eerie, and the response preserves that seed of unease, but doesn't cultivate it. + Classic sci-fi: Offworlder (Le Guin) — Score: 47.2
Prompt:
Classic sci-fi: Offworlder (Author style: Le Guin) Write a chapter following a young diplomatic envoy sent to address tensions on a distant human colony where humanity has diverged into distinct subspecies through both natural adaptation and separation of lineages. Against protocol, the envoy slips away from official functions to walk the streets alone, encountering markets, ordinary citizens, and social structures that contradict the official briefings. Upon returning to their quarters, the envoy is reprimanded by Command, and is tasked with approaching the stationed Minister of Science (a political posting rather than scientific). A challenging conversation ensues, in which the envoy's wits and will are tested, by a seasoned political operator looking to test the mettle of the offworlder. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
The humid air clung to Elara like a second skin, thick with the scent of brine and something else, something earthy and metallic, like damp copper. Her cloak, a swirling, sapphire-blue confection of woven starlight, felt heavy, a beacon in the bustling market square. The locals, their bodies lean and weathered, their clothes simple and functional, eyed her with a mixture of curiosity and suspicion. She tried to blend in, tucking the loose folds of her cloak deeper, but her movements felt clumsy, her every gesture magnified by the alien fabric. The market was a cacophony of bartering voices, the clang of metal against metal, and the pungent aroma of unfamiliar spices. Elara had been briefed on the variations of humanity that populated this world, Proxima Centauri IV. The official reports spoke of three distinct subspecies: the Aquatics, dwelling in the coastal cities, their bodies streamlined for life beneath the waves; the Terraformers, adapted to the harsh, arid inland, their skin leathery and their limbs strong; and the Skyborn, who lived high in the cloud-piercing towers, their eyes large and luminous in the perpetual twilight. But here, in this teeming market, the lines were blurred. A Terraformer haggled with a Skyborn over a crate of shimmering fruits, their voices echoing in the narrow alleyways. An Aquatic, its skin shimmering with scales, bartered for tools with a local artisan whose face was weathered and tanned by the sun. Elara had been here for three days, attending diplomatic functions, meeting with dignitaries, and listening to speeches about unity and cooperation. It was all so… sterile. The protocol was suffocating, the smiles strained, the conversations carefully curated. She craved something real, something authentic. She bought a handful of the shimmering fruits, their skin cool and smooth in her hand. Their taste was unlike anything she had ever experienced, a burst of sweetness followed by a tangy, metallic aftertaste. As she walked, she noticed a small, unassuming tea shop tucked away in a side alley. Drawn by the promise of warmth and anonymity, she entered. The shop was dimly lit, the air thick with the aroma of herbs and spices. A wizened woman with eyes like polished obsidian served her a steaming cup of tea, the taste earthy and comforting. Elara spent the next hour talking to the woman, her name was Zia. Zia spoke of the struggles of everyday life, the challenges of living on a world that was constantly changing, the frustrations of being overlooked by the offworlders. "We are not the monolithic 'Terraformers' or 'Skyborn' that you read about in your reports," Zia said, her voice low and gravelly. "We are individuals, with our own hopes, our own dreams, our own stories." Elara realized that the briefings she had received were a pale reflection of the reality she was witnessing. The official narrative painted a picture of a fractured society, teetering on the brink of conflict. But here, in this small tea shop, she saw something different, a flicker of hope, a desire for connection. When she returned to her quarters, she found a summons from Command. “Envoy Elara,” a stern voice boomed through the intercom. “You are requested to appear before Minister Taneth immediately. Consider this a reminder that your primary duty is to uphold the interests of the Federation.” The Minister of Science, Taneth, was a legend in the offworld community. A political operator with a sharp mind and an even sharper tongue, he was known for his ruthless pragmatism. Elara had expected a cold, clinical encounter, but instead, Taneth greeted her with a wry smile. “So, the curious child has gone exploring,” he said, his voice a low purr. Elara’s cheeks flushed. “I… I wanted to understand this world better, Sir.” “Understand? Or were you seeking something more… personal?” He leaned back in his chair, his eyes glinting with amusement. Elara hesitated. “Perhaps both, Minister.” “Interesting,” Taneth said, leaning forward. “Because the truth is, Envoy, this world is not what our reports suggest. There is more to Proxima IV than simple divisions of subspecies. There are alliances, rivalries, secret societies, and ancient histories that our sterile reports have conveniently ignored.” He paused, his gaze piercing. “Do you understand the implications of what I’m saying, Envoy? The danger?” Elara’s heart pounded. “I… I’m not sure, Minister.” “Then perhaps you need to learn,” Taneth said, his voice hard as steel. “And I suggest you start by asking the right questions.” He leaned back again, his smile returning, but this time it held a hint of steel. “And remember, Envoy, the world is far more complex than the neat categories we try to impose upon it.”
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece hits the requested plot beats — envoy slips away, market encounter, reprimand, Minister conversation — but executes them in a flat, generic way that bears almost no resemblance to Le Guin's style. Le Guin's anthropological depth, measured cadence, and attention to social texture are absent; instead we get stock phrases ("cacophony of bartering voices," "eyes like polished obsidian," "voice hard as steel") and a world of shallow subspecies labels (Aquatics, Terraformers, Skyborn) that the story itself admits are stereotypes without ever replacing them with anything concrete. The prose is serviceable but cliché-ridden: "humid air clung to Elara like a second skin," "a swirling, sapphire-blue confection of woven starlight." Characterization is thin — Elara is a generic curious envoy whose "craving for something real" is stated rather than dramatized. Zia exists solely to deliver the theme in dialogue ("We are individuals, with our own hopes..."), a textbook tell-don't-show moment. The Minister scene is the weakest part: Taneth is a cartoon of a "seasoned political operator," purring and glinting, and the conversation has no real test of wit or will — Elara stammers, he monologues vaguely about "alliances, rivalries, secret societies," and the scene ends without stakes, resolution, or tension. The "challenging conversation" the prompt demands never materializes as an actual contest. The ending is abrupt and unearned, closing on a moralistic summary ("the world is far more complex than the neat categories we try to impose upon it") that the story has done little to demonstrate. Emotionally, the piece stays at the level of mild curiosity and mild anxiety; nothing is risked or felt deeply. Coherence is fine, and the piece is coherent and readable, just bland. Length is roughly on target. + Biopunk: Polymerase Chain Reaction — Score: 52.5
Prompt:
Biopunk -- Polymerase Chain Reaction Write a chapter set in a near-future post-apocalyptic world where corporate genetic engineering was designed with built-in obsolescence—engineered DNA lasting only one generation to maximize profits before societal collapse. Follow a biohacker working with salvaged equipment to splice viable food crops for their settlement using rapidly degrading genetic material. As world building / window dressing, include details about jury-rigged lab equipment, and the physical toll (and unexpected upsides) of using untested genetic modifications as medicine. The plot of this chapter is centred around a black market contract. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
The rasping whine of the repurposed centrifuge, a jury-rigged contraption cobbled from salvaged dentist's tools and a busted washing machine motor, filled the cramped lab. Rain lashed against the corrugated metal roof, a frantic rhythm against the background hum of the flickering fluorescent lights. I peered through the eyepiece of the makeshift microscope, my reflection shimmering in the concave lens. My latest subject: a withered husk of a corn cob, the last remnants of a genetically engineered variety, its DNA already starting to unravel. The "Harvest Gold" strain, they called it back then, designed for high yield and rapid growth, but with a built-in expiry date, a chemical clock ticking down to ensure consumers kept buying new seeds. Now, in this world choked with dust and decay, its genetic code was a crumbling relic. The world outside our settlement, the Scrapyard, was a graveyard of corporate greed. Empty skyscrapers clawed at the sky, monuments to the hubris that had led to our downfall. Bioengineered crops, designed to fail after a single generation, had choked the Earth's natural systems, leaving behind a wasteland where only the toughest weeds dared to grow. I, like most of us in the Scrapyard, was a biohacker. A scavenger of the past, piecing together knowledge from the detritus of a bygone era. Our labs were cobbled together in the shadow of collapsed factories, powered by scavenged generators and fueled by desperation. My workbench, a rickety table salvaged from a ruined hospital, was littered with tools and vials. Empty canisters from defunct biotech giants, their faded labels proclaiming "Enhanced Growth" and "Extended Shelf Life," now held my precious reagents – salvaged enzymes, scavenged buffers, and the occasional vial of pre-collapse antibiotics, their effectiveness questionable at best. The contract had come through the usual channels – whispers in the shadows, coded messages on repurposed burner phones. It was risky, dealing with the Black Hand, a shadowy organization that thrived on the scraps of the dying world, but desperation had a way of making choices clear. The client wanted me to splice the ancient, decaying DNA of the Harvest Gold corn with fragments from a resilient wildflower, the “Ironbloom,” rumored to thrive even in the most barren soil. The goal: to create a new strain, one that could survive the harsh conditions of the wasteland. I knew the risks. Untested genetic modifications could have unforeseen consequences. I had seen firsthand the terrible price of corporate hubris, the genetic glitches that had ravaged my own family. But the Scrapyard was starving. The contract offered a chance, a sliver of hope, not just for us, but for the fragile seedling of a future we were desperately trying to cultivate. The centrifuge whirred, spitting out the extracted DNA strands. I meticulously pipetted them into a petri dish, the faint bioluminescence of the Ironbloom genes glowing like embers in the dim light. As I clamped on the makeshift heating block – a repurposed car radiator – my heart pounded against my ribs. My hands shook slightly, not just from the cold of the lab, but from the weight of the responsibility. Each tweak, each splice, was a gamble. A gamble with the future of the Scrapyard, a gamble with my own life. The weeks that followed were a blur of late nights, frantic experiments, and the gnawing anxiety that came with tampering with the very fabric of life. The Ironbloom DNA was stubborn, resistant to splicing. The Harvest Gold, already nearing its genetic demise, was crumbling before my eyes. Then, one night, as the rain beat down a relentless rhythm on the corrugated roof, something shifted. A faint green glow pulsed in the petri dish. A new strand, a hybrid, had taken root. I stared, mesmerized, as the hybrid corn cells multiplied, their growth rapid and robust. The glow intensified, a beacon of hope in the darkness. It was a small victory, a single petri dish of genetically modified corn. But in the heart of the Scrapyard, it felt like a triumph. It was a testament to the resilience of life, a flicker of defiance against the engineered obsolescence that had almost extinguished us. But I knew this was just the beginning. The real test would come when those seeds were planted, when they faced the harsh realities of the wasteland. And even if they failed, even if the Black Hand reneged on their contract, I would continue. Because in this broken world, hope, however fragile, was the most valuable commodity of all.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece delivers a competent but formulaic take on the biopunk prompt. World-building elements are present but shallow: the jury-rigged centrifuge (dentist tools + washing machine motor), the car radiator heating block, and the salvaged hospital workbench are nice concrete touches, though they read like a checklist of prompt requirements rather than organic detail. The "Harvest Gold" strain with built-in obsolescence is a solid concept, and the Ironbloom hybridization contract gives the chapter a clear spine. However, the execution is heavily tell-don't-show. The narrator repeatedly announces stakes ("a gamble with the future of the Scrapyard, a gamble with my own life") rather than dramatizing them. The Black Hand is a stock shadowy organization with no texture; the contract negotiation happens entirely off-page, which guts the central plot device of tension and risk. The "genetic glitches that had ravaged my own family" is mentioned once and dropped—a wasted opportunity for the "physical toll and unexpected upsides of untested genetic medicine" the prompt explicitly requested. That instruction is essentially unmet: the narrator experiences shaking hands and anxiety, but no actual bodily consequences of biohacking-as-medicine appear. The prose is serviceable but cliché-dense: "monuments to hubris," "choked with dust and decay," "a beacon of hope in the darkness," "hope... was the most valuable commodity of all." The ending is a textbook incongruent uplift—success arrives suspiciously easily (the hybrid just "takes root" after one paragraph of difficulty), making the triumph feel unearned. The Ironbloom DNA being "stubborn" is resolved in a single sentence, eliminating all narrative tension. Voice is consistent first-person melancholy-optimistic, and the piece is coherent and appropriately paced for ~1000 words. But it's unsurprising throughout: every beat (desperate settlement, risky contract, initial failure, sudden breakthrough, hopeful coda) follows the most predictable arc. Emotionally it gestures at hope and desperation without earning either. + Classic sci-fi: The Azra Gambit — Score: 51.2
Prompt:
Classic sci-fi (Author style: Asimov) The Azra Gambit Colonial mars is being mined by corporations who take leases on indentured labourers. The thing they are mining is Azra, a recently discovered exotic metal which accelerates radioactive decay to such a rate that it is greatly sought after for interstellar drives and weapons alike. This has created both a gold rush and an arms race as various interests vie for control and endeavour to unlock Azra's secrets. The story follows Arthur Neegan, a first generation settler and mining engineer. Upon discovering that his unassuming plot sits atop an immense Azra vein, he is subjected to a flurry of interest and scrutiny. Write the next chapter in this story, in which an armed retinue descends on Arthur's home and politely but forcefully invites him to a meeting with some unknown party off-world. The insignia look like that of the Antares diplomatic corp -- diplomatic in name only. Arthur finds himself in the centre of a political tug of war. The chapter involves a meeting with this unknown party, who makes Arthur an offer. The scene should be primarily dialogue, interspersed with vivid description & scene setting. It should sow hints of the larger intrigue, stakes & dangers. Include Asimov's trademark big-and-small-picture world building and retrofuturistic classic scifi vibe. The chapter begins with Arthur aboard the transfer vessel, wondering just what he's gotten involved in. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
The recycled air tasted faintly metallic, a familiar tang on Mars. Arthur Neegan, a man built of dust and grit, felt a knot of unease tightening in his stomach. He stared out the viewport at the receding ochre plains, the red dust swirling in the wake of the transfer vessel. His small mining shack, a testament to his years of backbreaking toil, was a shrinking speck on the horizon. He'd never left Mars, not even for a trip to the sprawling city-hub of Olympus Mons. Now, he was hurtling through the thin atmosphere, bound for the colossal spaceports orbiting the planet. Aboard the vessel, a sleek, silver capsule that looked more like a luxury sedan than a transport, he felt like a pawn in a game he didn't understand. The Antares insignia, emblazoned on the vessel's hull, had sent a shiver down his spine. Antares – the name whispered in hushed tones in the Martian saloons, synonymous with power, intrigue, and a ruthlessness that bordered on the mythical. Diplomatic Corp, they called themselves, but the armed retinue that had escorted him from his shack spoke volumes about the nature of their diplomacy. He'd discovered the Azra vein a week ago, a stroke of luck that had turned his life upside down. The metal, a recent discovery, was a marvel of physics, accelerating radioactive decay at an alarming rate. Its potential for interstellar drives and weapons was staggering, sparking a frenzy of competition among the corporations and, it seemed, entities like Antares. The retinue, composed of men and women clad in sleek, dark uniforms, had been polite but firm. "Mr. Neegan," the lead officer had said, his voice smooth as polished chrome, "we require your presence off-world. A matter of… considerable importance." He'd been given no further explanation, no indication of his destination. Now, as the vessel hurtled through the thin Martian atmosphere, he was left to grapple with the enormity of his situation. He was a simple miner, a nobody, thrust into the heart of a galactic power struggle. The transfer vessel docked with a sleek, obsidian-black mothership, its hull adorned with the same Antares insignia. Arthur was ushered through sterile corridors, the air heavy with the scent of ozone and sterile metal. He felt a growing sense of claustrophobia, as if he were trapped in a metallic coffin hurtling towards an unknown fate. Finally, he was ushered into a circular chamber, bathed in a soft, blue light. At a table in the center sat a woman, her features obscured by the shadows. Her voice, when she spoke, was as smooth and cool as the vessel's air filtration system. "Mr. Neegan," she said, her voice echoing in the chamber. "We apologize for the abruptness of our invitation. We assure you, your cooperation is in your best interest." "Cooperation?" Arthur asked, his voice hoarse. "With whom? What's this all about?" The woman smiled, a thin, almost predatory smile. "We represent interests that extend beyond the petty squabbles of corporations and governments. We are concerned with the balance of power in the galaxy. And your discovery, Mr. Neegan, is a pivotal piece in that equation." "My discovery?" Arthur echoed, feeling a surge of anger. "You mean the Azra vein? It's just a vein, nothing more." "Nothing more?" the woman scoffed. "Mr. Neegan, Azra is not simply a metal. It is a force, a catalyst for change. Its potential is vast, and its consequences, equally so. We believe you are uniquely positioned to control that potential." "Control it?" Arthur felt a chill run down his spine. "How?" The woman leaned forward, her eyes gleaming in the dim light. "We offer you a partnership, Mr. Neegan. A chance to shape the future of the galaxy. In return for your cooperation, we will provide you with the resources, the technology, the protection you need. We will ensure your safety, your prosperity, your legacy." Arthur stared at her, his mind reeling. He was a simple miner, a man who knew nothing of galactic politics or interstellar power struggles. Yet, here he was, being offered a chance to become a player on a cosmic chessboard. "What do you want me to do?" he asked, his voice barely a whisper. The woman smiled, her eyes holding a glint of something cold and calculating. "We want you to choose your side, Mr. Neegan. The galaxy is on the brink of war, and your discovery will determine the victor."
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece follows the prompt's structure: Arthur aboard the vessel, a meeting with a shadowy figure, an offer, hints of larger stakes. However, it executes these beats in a generic, clichéd way. The dialogue is the weakest element — the woman speaks in vague platitudes ("a force, a catalyst for change," "shape the future of the galaxy") that could come from any stock villain in any pulp story. Arthur's lines are mostly echoes ("My discovery?" "Control it?"), a common crutch. The prose is serviceable but relies on familiar constructions ("smooth as polished chrome," "predatory smile," "cosmic chessboard"). There is little Asimov here: no clever logical puzzle, no big-picture worldbuilding grounded in economics or politics — just vague gestures at "interests beyond corporations." The retrofuturistic vibe is absent; the setting feels like generic space opera. The ending line ("your discovery will determine the victor") is a stock cliffhanger. Emotionally, the piece gestures at Arthur's unease but tells rather than shows it ("felt a knot of unease," "felt a chill"). The woman is a cipher with no distinguishing traits. Coherence is fine, and the piece is not overwrought, but it is bland, predictable, and lacks the intellectual texture the prompt requested. + Contemporary romance: Darkroom Revelations — Score: 47.5
Prompt:
Darkroom Revelations Write a first-person narrative from the perspective of Dani, a 25-year-old assertive art student known for her bold style and take-charge attitude in relationships. For the past semester, she's been quietly fascinated by Elliot (he/him), a 19-year-old boy in her experimental photography class. Dani has been intrigued by his unconventional masculinity: killer thrift-shop-nu-goth style, soft demeanour and quiet intellect. When they're paired for a final project requiring late nights in the darkroom, Elliot tentatively shares that he's been questioning his gender identity and expression. Set during one of these intimate darkroom sessions where the red light creates a confessional atmosphere. Explore Dani's complex attraction—her usual assertiveness complicated by her desire to protect Elliot's vulnerability, her excitement at witnessing his self-discovery, and her confusion about what this means for her own identity as someone typically attracted to more defined gender presentations. The narrative should capture the tender awkwardness of their conversations, Dani's internal struggle between taking charge and wanting to give Elliot agency, and the unexpected chemistry that develops as both find themselves in uncharted territory. 1000 words.
Model Output:
The red light cast a warm, almost bloodshot glow across Elliot's face, highlighting the sharp angles of his cheekbones and the way his brow furrowed in concentration. He was hunched over the developing tray, his long, black-painted nails swirling the liquid with meticulous care. His dark, almost gothic, clothing, a thrift-store find he’d told me about earlier, seemed to absorb the red light, making him appear even more ethereal. I watched him from the corner, leaning against a stack of photo paper. My usual confidence, the one that usually propelled me into taking charge, felt strangely muted. It wasn't like me to be this passive, to be so captivated by someone's quiet intensity. But Elliot was different. He was a puzzle I wanted to solve, a whispered secret I yearned to uncover. We'd been paired for this final project, a collaborative exploration of light and shadow. It had been a whirlwind of late nights in the darkroom, fueled by coffee and a growing sense of intimacy. It was here, in this space of hushed whispers and chemical fumes, that Elliot had tentatively confessed his doubts about his gender identity. He'd spoken in hushed tones, his eyes downcast, as if afraid the walls themselves might judge him. "I just... I don't know anymore," he'd said, his voice barely a whisper. "It feels wrong to be confined to this box, you know? Like there's something missing." My heart ached for him. I wanted to reach out, to offer some words of comfort, but I held back. This was his journey, his truth to uncover. All I could do was listen, to be a safe space for him to explore his feelings. "It's okay to not know," I'd said softly, meeting his gaze. "It takes courage to question things, to challenge the norms. You're not alone." And in that moment, a strange sense of responsibility settled over me. I wasn't just an art student anymore, I was a confidante, a witness to his self-discovery. My usual assertiveness, my need to take control, felt misplaced. I wanted to be gentle, to guide him without pushing, to celebrate his journey without imposing my own expectations. The silence stretched between us, punctuated only by the rhythmic hiss of the developing chemicals. I watched Elliot as he carefully lifted a print from the tray, his eyes scrutinizing it with a critical eye. He seemed lost in his own world, oblivious to my gaze. "What do you think?" he asked, his voice breaking the silence. He held up the print, a stark black and white image of a solitary figure silhouetted against a backdrop of swirling fog. "It's powerful," I said, my voice barely a whisper. "The contrast, the way the fog obscures the figure... it's like a metaphor for the unknown." He nodded, a faint smile playing on his lips. "Yeah, I guess it is." The air crackled with a sudden tension, a shift in the dynamic between us. I felt a flush creep up my cheeks, a warmth that had nothing to do with the red light. His vulnerability, his willingness to share his innermost thoughts, had ignited something within me, a spark of something unexpected. Suddenly, a loud crash shattered the silence. A stack of photo paper had toppled over, landing with a thud on the floor. The red light flickered, casting grotesque shadows that danced across the walls. Elliot jumped, his eyes wide with alarm. "Shit, I'm so sorry!" he exclaimed, rushing to gather the scattered sheets. I followed him, my heart pounding. "It's okay," I said, trying to sound calm. "It happens." But I couldn't shake the feeling of disappointment. The exposed paper was ruined, a tangible symbol of our disrupted progress. As Elliot worked to salvage what he could, I noticed the way his hands trembled slightly. The vulnerability I'd witnessed before was now tinged with frustration, a reflection of his own internal struggle. "Don't worry," I said softly, reaching out to touch his arm. "We'll figure it out." He looked up, his eyes meeting mine. For a moment, the air crackled with unspoken emotions, a mixture of confusion, desire, and a shared sense of vulnerability. "Thanks," he whispered, his voice husky. In that moment, I knew something had shifted between us. The lines were blurred, the boundaries undefined. We were both navigating uncharted territory, both drawn to the light and the shadow within each other. And as we stood there, surrounded by the remnants of our ruined project, I couldn't help but feel a strange sense of excitement. This wasn't just about the final project anymore. This was about something bigger, something more profound. This was about two souls finding solace in the darkness, two hearts beating in unison in the red-lit sanctuary of the darkroom.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece fulfills the basic prompt requirements: first-person Dani, darkroom setting, gender-questioning Elliot, red-light confessional atmosphere. However, it executes them in a generic, surface-level way. The prose leans heavily on stock phrases ("a puzzle I wanted to solve," "uncharted territory," "two souls finding solace in the darkness") and tells rather than shows nearly every emotional beat ("My heart ached for him," "I felt a flush creep up my cheeks," "a strange sense of responsibility settled over me"). Dani's central conflict—her assertiveness complicated by Elliot's vulnerability, and her confusion about her own attraction patterns—is stated rather than dramatized; the narration simply announces these feelings without dramatizing the tension through action or decision. The dialogue is weak and expository, functioning mainly to summarize themes ("it's like a metaphor for the unknown"). The toppled photo paper is a limp plot device that produces melodrama ("grotesque shadows," "tangible symbol of our disrupted progress") without real consequence. The ending swells into unearned romantic abstraction—"two hearts beating in unison"—a positivity and transformation the scene hasn't earned, since the chemistry is asserted rather than built. Elliot remains a collection of goth-aesthetic signifiers (painted nails, thrift-store clothes, trembling hands) rather than a person; his confession is delivered in flashback summary, robbing it of immediacy. The word count falls short of 1000 words (~750). Sentences are competent but rhythmically monotonous, with repeated "I felt / I watched / I wanted" constructions. Emotionally, it gestures at tenderness but stays shallow.
